DULANG 6A PRODUCTION TEST RESULTS

SUMMARY

Two production tests were successfully conducted at Dulang 6A on
November 1-9, 1989. Test 1 was on the E-42, Test 2 was on the £E-24,
and Test 2A was on the commingled £-24 and E-26 reservoirs. Each
test averaged about 3 days with minimal trouble and downtime losses.
The objectives of the tests were to confirm the presence of
hydrocarbons, measure the zone’s productivity, determine the
reservoir’s characteristics, and obtain fluid samples. Note that as
the hole was a sidetrack, all measured depths, which this report is
based on, were corrected for an average hole deviation of 8.59 to
determine the true vertical depths.

The following conclusions can be made:
TEST 1 (E-42 ZONE)

The E-42 which was perforated from 1446.0 to 1451.0 m. is oi]
bearing. Average oil flow rate during the major flow period through
a 96/64-inch choke was 635 STBPD. Associated GOR was 494 SCF/STB.
The FWHP and FBHP were 120 psig and 629 psia, respectively. No water
or sand was produced. The zone’s productivity is estimated to be
0.46 STB/D/psi. The initial formation pressure is 2033 psia at
1448.0 m. (from RFT). The maximum recorded BHT was 2249 F. The
formation has a permeability of 37 md., for a net thickness of 1.7
m. (copy of logs is attached in the folder}, and a skin factor of 0.
The crude has a gravity of 35.39 APE and is waxy with a pour point
of about 989 F. The solution gas contains about 52 % CO2. These
results are also summarized in Table 1.

DULGREP/4
TEST 2 (E-24 ZONE)

The E-24 which was perforated from 1319.0 to 1326.0 m. is oil
bearing. Average o71 flow rate during the major flow period through
a 32/64-inch choke was 525 STBPD. Associated GOR was 764 SCF/STB.
The FWHP and FBHP were 150 psig and 609 psia, respectively. No water
was produced. The zone’s productivity is estimated to be 0.41
STB/D/psi. The initial formation pressure is 1890 psia at 1322.5 m..
The maximum recorded BHT for the zone was 2089 F. The formation has
a permeability of 28 md., for a net thickness of 1.4 m., and a_ skin
factor of 0. The crude has a gravity of 41.09 API and is slightly
waxy (pour point of 809 F). The solution gas contains 51 % C02.

TEST 2A (E-24 AND E-26 ZONES)

The commingled E-24 and E-26 zones produced both oil and water. The
combined oil flow rate was 1779 STBPD through a 96/64-inch choke.
Associated GOR was 437 SCF/STB. The FWHP and FBHP were 250 psig and
1022 psia, respectively. The water which was produced at a water-cut
of about 6 % is believed to have been produced from the bottom of
the E-26 zone. The combined zones have an average permeability of 55
md., assuming a net thickness of 5.2 m. The mixed crude has gravity
of 34.49 API and a pour point of 949 F. The CO? content of the gas
is 56 %.

TEST PROCEDURE

With the wel] shut-in, each zone was perforated with Schlumberger’s
2-1/8 inch Enerjet through tubing perforating gun (6 spf. at zero
degrees phasing). The zones were perforated with a _ slight
underbalance of about 250 psi with diesel in the tubing. Surface
opening and shut-in methods were used. Wellhead pressure was bled to
zero before perforation to ensure maximum differential into the
wellbore. The well remained shut-in after perforation during the
period when the gun was retrieved and while the pressure gauge
assembly was run in the hole.

DUL6REP/5
A typical dual flow dual shut-in test procedure was used. After
about 5-10 minutes cof initial flow, the well was shut-in for = an
inital buildup. The well was later opened for a major flow that was
of sufficient duration for adequate cleanup and stabilized flowing
conditions to prevail so that a good assessment of the well’s
productivity can be made and representative surface samples can be
collected. A 2 MBTU heater was used downstream of the choke manifold
to avoid accumulation of wax in surface equipment of the high pour
point Dulang crude. After the major flow, the well was shut-in for a
major buildup period that was of sufficient duration to establish a
semilog straight line in the middle time region. A gradient survey
was run at the end of the buildup before terminating the test.

During the major flow, periodic surface samples were taken at the
choke manifold for on-site analysis. This includes determination of
BS&W content; concentrations of H2S, C02, and Cy, through C5
components in the gas stream; gas gravity, API, pour point, and
water resistivity, chlorides, and pH. Samples for detailed
laboratory analysis including duplicate sets of gas, oi], and water
were collected from the separator anda detailed list of those
collected are summarized in Table 2. The pressurized oi] samples
were collected by mercury displacement, while the gas was collected
in evacuated cylinders. No bottom hole samples were taken from any
of the zones. Due to the poor rock quality, the reservoirs required
large differential to produce (causing the BHP to fall below the
bubble point). Therefore, a representative bottomhole sample could
not be collected.

TEST EQUIPMENT

A schematic of the bottomhole test assembly is shown in Figure 1. It
consists a mule shoe, a nipple, seal assembly, a locator sub, a
permanent packer, and a 3-1/2 inch tubing string. The upper test
assembly consists of Flopetrol’s sub-sea test tree, sub-sea
lubricator valve, contro] header, wireline BOP, and the wireline
lubricator. The surface equipment (Figure 2) consists of choke
manifold, diesel-fired heater, a three-phase separator, two
DUL6REP/6
50-barrel test tanks, and a transfer pump. A heater was essential as
the crude, especially from the E-42 and E-26, has high pour point,
about 98°F. Flowlines to flare had the option of being either routed
to the port or starboard side of the floater (semi-submersible).

Wellhead pressures were monitored with a dead-weight tester and with
an Amerada pressure gauge serving as a spare. Bottomhole pressures
and temperatures were monitored with Flopetrol’s gauges. Two types
of pressure gauges were used (a quartz (CRG) and a strain (SSDP)
which was used as a spare) with readings from the quartz displayed
in real-time and used for all analysis. A sketch of the layout of
the pressure gauge assembly is shown in Figure 3.

DISCUSSION OF RESULTS

TEST 1 (E-42 ZONE)

No major problems or downtime losses occurred while testing this
zone, except that the zone required a second set of perforations to
help initiate flow. Details of the chronology of events and data
collected during the test are included in Appendix A.

After 2 hours of the major flow, the production rate had dropped to
about 20 STBPD. To help explain the low production rate, we decided
to re-correlate the perforation depth to ensure that they were
located at the correct interval. The results from the correlation
run showed the perforations were on depth. We then suspected that
the perforations may not have successfully penetrated the casing and
decided to perforate over the same interval. Note that earlier
inspection of the perforating gun from the first set of perforations
revealed that all the shots had been fired. After reperforation, the
well continued to produce at a low rate, for about 3 hours, before
it came in. By this time it was nightfall and the well was shut-in
once gas surfaced for the first time, for safety reasons. The well
continued to exhibit slugging throughout the flow period even after
it was considered cleaned up due to the relatively large volume of
gas liberated in the wellbore.

DUL6REP/7
The results of the Horner interpretation for the initial buildup is
shown in Figure 4. An anomaly was observed over the last few data
points. They continued to increase sharply which could be attributed
to communication from below behind the casing. A packer leak (casing
pressure was constant) and the possible movement of the pressure
gauge (wireline was packed off at the stuffing box) were ruled out.
It could not be due to phase segregation in the wellbore as the last
recorded pressure of 2155 psia was significantly above the estimated
formation pressure of about 2033 psia at 1448.0 m. determined from
RFT. Extrapolating a possible MTR straight line yields a p* of 2023
psia at 1441.0 m., the location of the pressure gauge in wellbore,
or 2036 psia at 1448.5 m. (mid-perforation) in the reservoir. Note
that this pressure is slightly higher than that obtained from the
RFT at 1448.0 m. (2033 psia), which could be easily attributed to
the uncertainty in the choice of the correct MTR straight line. The
volume below the packer was still assumed to be completely filled
with drilling mud of 10.9 ppg.

The Horner plot from the major buildup is shown in Figure 5. The
producing time for the Horner analysis was estimated from the
cumulative production. This plot yields a p* of 2022 psia or 2030
psia at 1448.5 m. which is in reasonable agreement with that from
the initial buildup (vs.2036 psia). A measured reservoir gradient of
0.33 psi/ft was used for the correction. The MTR slope yields a
permeability of 37 md., for a net thickness of 1.7 m., and a skin
factor of essentially 0. Table 3 lists a summary of the parameters
used and results of the transient analysis. Note that no anomaly was
seen on this plot, unlike that of the initial buildup.

Figure 6 shows the type curve fit. The Type Curve analysis yields a
permeability of 46 md. and a skin factor of OQ which are in
reasonable agreement with those from the Horner analysis. Table 4
lists the summary of the results for this analysis.

DUL6REP/8
TEST 2 (E-24 ZONE)

For this test, to avoid potential charging of the reservoir (tight)
while running the wireline into the hole, a volume equal to the
volume of the wireline and the pressure gauge assembly(approximately
11 gallons) was removed from the wellbore while running the pressure
gauge assembly into the hole. Details of the events and data
collected during this test is included in Appendix B.

After about 2 hours in the major flow, when the production rate had
dropped to about 20 STBPD, we decided to retrieve the pressure
gauges and commence swabbing the well. After 20 swabs runs and
recovering a cumulative volume of about 23 bbis (diesel), the well
came in. The well was then shut-in to rig down the swabbing
equipment and while the pressure gauge assembly was being run in the
hole. Like the E-42, this zone also exhibited continued slugging
throughout the flow period, even after reasonable cleanup.

The Horner plot from the initial buildup is shown in Figure 7. An
anomaly was observed over the later data points in that they
continued to increase sharply. It is believed, that this anomaly is
attributed to a pressure hump from phase segregation in the
wellbore. Extrapolating a possible MIR straight line yields a p* of
1874 psia at 1314.0 m. (the location of the pressure gauge) which
corresponds to 1890 psia at 1322.5 m. (corrected with 10.9 ppg mud
gradient). Note that RFT pressure measured was 1890 psia at 1322.0m.

The Horner plot from the major buildup is shown in Figure 8. No
anomaly was observed on this buildup and a well defined MIR was
established. Extrapolating a straight line passing through the MIR
yields a p* of 1881 psia at 1314.0 m. or 1890 psia at 1322.5 Mm.
which is excellent agreement with that obtained from the RFT. A
measured reservoir gradient of 0.31 psi/ft was used for the
correction. Analysis for the reservoir characteristics from the MTR
slope yields a permeability of 28 md., for a net thickness of 1.4
m., and a skin factor of essentially 0 (Table 5).

FIgure 9 shows a plot of the Type Curve match. This analysis yields
a permeability of 44 md. (Table 6), which is in reasonable good
agreement with that from the Horner analysis.

TEST 2A (COMMINGLED E-24, E-26 ZONES)

To save rig-time, the E-26 zone was commingle tested with the E-24
zone. Due to the long perforation interval from 1342.0 to 1356.5 m.,
two perforation runs had to be made. To achieve underbalance
perforation, the well was flowed for about 1/2 hour to draw down the
bottomhole pressure before perforating. The well was shut-in just
before perforation and for a short period thereafter to avoid
perforation debris from being flushed to the surface and potentially
clogging the choke. This same procedure was repeated for the second
perforation run.

Details of the chronology of events and data collected during the
test are included in Appendix C. A minor problem, incurring about 2
hours trouble time, occured when the production logging tool (PLT)
assembly had to be retrieved to the surface to replace the
electrical connection at the tool’s head. The test procedure for
this commingled zone was modified to consist of only a major flow
and a major shut-in. The spinner survey was made to determine the
split in the flow rates between the two zones. As the pressure gauge
was in continuous motion during the spinner survey, no bottomhole
pressures were acquired, except later at the end for a buildup. Four
cycles of passes were made with the spinner and a copy of the log is
included in the folder.

DUL6REP/10
The Horner plot of the buildup is shown in Figure 10. A well defined
MTR was established and extrapolating a straight line through this
region yields a p* of 1915 psia at 1312.0 m. (the location of the
pressure gauge for this test). Since it is not possible to relate
this pressure to either of the zones, no attempt was made to correct
this pressure to a datum. The slope of the line yields a
permeability to oil of 55 md. (Table 7) for the combined zones
(assumed a net thickness of 1.4 plus 3.8m. for the E-24 and E-26
zones, respectively}. This corresponds to a permeability to oi] (6 %
water was also produced) of 68 md. for the E-26 zone alone. The skin
factor is essentially 0. A reservoir gradient of 0.33 psi/ft for
the crude and 0.39 psi/ft for water (below 1339.0 m.) were obtained
from the gradient survey at the end of the buildup.

A plot of the Type Curve match is shown in Figure 11. This analysis
yields a permeability (for the combined zones) of 79 md. and a_ skin
factor of 0 (Table 8) which are in reasonable agreement with the
Horner analysis.

DULANG 6A PRODUCTION TEST RESULTS

SUMMARY

Two production tests were successfully conducted at Dulang 6A on
November 1-9, 1989. Test 1 was on the E-42, Test 2 was on the E-24,
and Test 2A was on the commingled E-24 and E-26 reservoirs. Each
test averaged about 3 days with minimal trouble and downtime losses.
The objectives of the tests were to confirm the presence of
hydrocarbons, measure the zone’s productivity, determine the
reservoir’s characteristics, and obtain fluid samples. Note that as
the hole was a sidetrack, all measured depths, which this report is
based on, were corrected for an average hole deviation of 8.59 to
determine the true vertical depths.

The following conclusions can be made:
TEST 1 (E-42 ZONE)

The E-42 which was perforated from 1446.0 to 1451.0 m. is oil
bearing. Average oil flow rate during the major flow period through
a 96/64-inch choke was 635 STBPD. Associated GOR was 494 SCF/STB.
The FWHP and FBHP were 120 psig and 629 psia, respectively. No water
or sand was produced. The zone’s productivity is estimated to be
0.46 STB/D/psi. The initial formation pressure is 2033 psia at
1448.0 m. (from RFT). The maximum recorded BHT was 2249 F. The
formation has a permeability of 37 md., for a net thickness of 1.7
m. (copy of logs is attached in the folder), and a skin factor of 0.
The crude has a gravity of 35.39 API and is waxy with a pour point
of about 98° F. The solution gas contains about 52 % C02. These
results are also summarized in Table 1.
TEST 2 (E-24 ZONE)

The £-24 which was perforated from 1319.0 to 1326.0 m. is oil
bearing. Average oi] flow rate during the major flow period through
a 32/64-inch choke was 525 STBPD. Associated GOR was 764 SCF/STB.
The FWHP and FBHP were 150 psig and 609 psia, respectively. No water
was produced. The zone’s productivity is estimated to be 0.41
STB/D/psi. The initial formation pressure is 1890 psia at 1322.5 m..
The maximum recorded BHT for the zone was 2089 F. The formation § has
a permeability of 28 md., for a net thickness of 1.4 m., and a_ skin
factor of 0. The crude has a gravity of 41.09 API and is’ slightly
waxy (pour point of 80° F). The solution gas contains 51 % C02.

TEST 2A (E-24 AND E-26 ZONES)

The commingled E-24 and E-26 zones produced both oi] and water. The
combined oi] flow rate was 1779 STBPD through a 96/64-inch choke.
Associated GOR was 437 SCF/STB. The FWHP and FBHP were 250 psig and
1022 psia, respectively. The water which was produced at a water-cut
of about 6 % is believed to have been produced from the bottom of
the E-26 zone. The combined zones have an average permeability of 55
md., assuming a net thickness of 5.2 m. The mixed crude has gravity
of 34.49 API and a pour point of 94° F. The CO? content of the gas
is 56 %.

TEST PROCEDURE

With the well shut-in, each zone was perforated with Schlumberger’s
2-1/8 inch Enerjet through tubing perforating gun (6 spf. at zero
degrees phasing). The zones were perforated with a _ slight
underbalance of about 250 psi with diesel in the tubing. Surface
opening and shut-in methods were used. Wellhead pressure was bled to
zero before perforation to ensure maximum differential into the
wellbore. The well remained shut-in after perforation during the
period when the gun was retrieved and while the pressure gauge
assembly was run in the hole.
A typical dual flow dual shut-in test procedure was used. After
about 5-10 minutes of initial flow, the well was shut-in for an
inital buildup. The well was later opened for a major flow that was
of sufficient duration for adequate cleanup and stabilized flowing
conditions to prevail so that a good assessment of the well’s
productivity can be made and representative surface samples can be
collected. A 2 MBTU heater was used downstream of the choke manifold
to avoid accumulation of wax in surface equipment of the high pour
point Dulang crude. After the major flow, the well was shut-in for a
major buildup period that was of sufficient duration to establish a
semilog straight line in the middle time region. A gradient survey
was run at the end of the buildup before terminating the test.

During the major flow, periodic surface samples were taken at the
choke manifold for on-site analysis. This includes determination of
BS&W content; concentrations of H2S, C02, and Cy through C5
components in the gas stream; gas gravity, API, pour point, and
water resistivity, chlorides, and pH. Samples for detailed
laboratory analysis including duplicate sets of gas, oil, and water
were collected from the separator anda detailed list of those
collected are summarized in Table 2. The pressurized oil samples
were collected by mercury displacement, while the gas was collected
in evacuated cylinders. No bottom hole samples were taken from any
of the zones. Due to the poor rock quality, the reservoirs required
large differential to produce {causing the BHP to fall below the
bubble point). Therefore, a representative bottomhole sample could
not be collected.

TEST EQUIPMENT

A schematic of the bottomhole test assembly is shown in Figure 1. It
consists a mule shoe, a nipple, seal assembly, a locator sub, a
permanent packer, and a 3-1/2 inch tubing string. The upper test
assembly consists of Flopetrol’s sub-sea test tree,  sub-sea
lubricator valve, control header, wireline BOP, and the wireline
lubricator. The surface equipment (Figure 2) consists of choke
manifold, diesel-fired heater, a three-phase separator, two
DUL6REP/6
50-barrel test tanks, and a transfer pump. A heater was essential as
the crude, especially from the E-42 and E-26, has high pour point,
about 98°F. Flowlines to flare had the option of being either routed
to the port or starboard side of the floater (semi-submersible).

Wellhead pressures were monitored with a dead-weight tester and with
an Amerada pressure gauge serving as a spare. Bottomhole pressures
and temperatures were monitored with Flopetrol’s gauges. Two types
of pressure gauges were used (a quartz (CRG) and a strain (SSDP)
which was used as a spare) with readings from the quartz displayed
in real-time and used for all analysis. A sketch of the layout of
the pressure gauge assembly is shown in Figure 3.

DISCUSSION OF RESULTS

TEST 1 (E-42 ZONE)

No major problems or downtime losses occurred while testing this
zone, except that the zone required a second set of perforations to
help initiate flow. Details of the chronology of events and data
collected during the test are included in Appendix A.

After 2 hours of the major flow, the production rate had dropped to
about 20 STBPD. To help explain the low production rate, we decided
to re-correlate the perforation depth to ensure that they were
located at the correct interval. The results from the correlation
run showed the perforations were on depth. We then suspected that
the perforations may not have successfully penetrated the casing and
decided to perforate over the same interval. Note that earlier
inspection of the perforating gun from the first set of perforations
revealed that all the shots had been fired. After reperforation, the
well continued to produce at a Jow rate, for about 3 hours, before
it came in. By this time it was nightfall and the well was shut-in
once gas surfaced for the first time, for safety reasons. The well
continued to exhibit slugging throughout the flow period even after
it was considered cleaned up due to the relatively large volume of
gas liberated in the wellbore.

DUL6REP/7
The results of the Horner interpretation for the initial buildup is
shown in Figure 4. An anomaly was observed over the last few data
points. They continued to increase sharply which could be attributed
to communication from below behind the casing. A packer leak (casing
pressure was constant) and the possible movement of the pressure
gauge (wireline was packed off at the stuffing box) were ruled out.
It could not be due to phase segregation in the wellbore as the last
recorded pressure of 2155 psia was significantly above the estimated
formation pressure of about 2033 psia at 1448.0 m. determined from
RFT. Extrapolating a possible MTR straight line yields a p* of 2023
psia at 1441.0 m., the location of the pressure gauge in wellbore,
or 2036 psia at 1448.5 m. (mid-perforation) in the reservoir. Note
that this pressure is slightly higher than that obtained from the
RFT at 1448.0 m. (2033 psia), which could be easily attributed to
the uncertainty in the choice of the correct MIR straight line. The
volume below the packer was still assumed to be completely filled
with drilling mud of 10.9 ppg.

The Horner plot from the major buildup is shown in Figure 5. The
producing time for the Horner analysis was estimated from the
cumulative production. This plot yields a p* of 2022 psia or 2030
psia at 1448.5 m. which is in reasonable agreement with that from
the initial buildup (vs.2036 psia). A measured reservoir gradient of
0.33 psi/ft was used for the correction. The MIR slope yields a
permeability of 37 md., for a net thickness of 1.7 m., and a skin
factor of essentially 0. Table 3 lists a summary of the parameters
used and results of the transient analysis. Note that no anomaly was
seen on this plot, unlike that of the initial buildup.

Figure 6 shows the type curve fit. The Type Curve analysis yields a
permeability of 46 md. and a skin factor of 0O which are in
reasonable agreement with those from the Horner analysis. Table 4
lists the summary of the results for this analysis.
TEST 2 (E-24 ZONE)

For this test, to avoid potential charging of the reservoir (tight)
while running the wireline into the hole, a volume equal to the
volume of the wireline and the pressure gauge assembly(approximately
11 gallons) was removed from the wellbore while running the pressure
gauge assembly into the hole. Details of the events and data
collected during this test is included in Appendix B.

After about 2 hours in the major flow, when the production rate had
dropped to about 20 STBPD, we decided to retrieve the pressure
gauges and commence swabbing the well. After 20 swabs runs and
recovering a cumulative volume of about 23 bbls (diesel), the well
came in. The well was then shut-in to rig down the swabbing
equipment and while the pressure gauge assembly was being run in the
hole. Like the E-42, this zone also exhibited continued slugging
throughout the flow period, even after reasonable cleanup.

The Horner plot from the initial buildup is shown in Figure 7. An
anomaly was observed over the later data points in that they
continued to increase sharply. It is believed, that this anomaly is
attributed to a pressure hump from phase segregation in the
wellbore. Extrapolating a possible MIR straight line yields a p* of
1874 psia at 1314.0 m. (the location of the pressure gauge) which
corresponds to 1890 psia at 1322.5 m. (corrected with 10.9 ppg mud
gradient). Note that RFT pressure measured was 1890 psia at 1322.0m.

The Horner plot from the major buildup is shown in Figure 8. No
anomaly was observed on this buildup and a well defined MTR was
established. Extrapolating a straight line passing through the MIR
yields a p* of 1881 psia at 1314.0 m. or 1890 psia at 1322.5 om.
which is excellent agreement with that obtained from the RFT. A
measured reservoir gradient of 0.31 psi/ft was used for the
correction. Analysis for the reservoir characteristics from the MTR
slope yields a permeability of 28 md., for a net thickness of 1.4
m., and a skin factor of essentially 0 (Table 5).

Figure 9 shows a plot of the Type Curve match. This analysis yields
a permeability of 44 md. (Table 6), which is in reasonable good
agreement with that from the Horner analysis.

TEST 2A (COMMINGLED E-24, E-26 ZONES)

To save rig-time, the E-26 zone was commingle tested with the E-24
zone. Due to the long perforation interval from 1342.0 to 1356.5 m.,
two perforation runs had to be made. To achieve underbalance
perforation, the well was flowed for about 1/2 hour to draw down the
bottomhole pressure before perforating. The wel] was shut-in just
before perforation and for a short period thereafter to avoid
perforation debris from being flushed to the surface and potentially
clogging the choke. This same procedure was repeated for the second
perforation run.

Details of the chronology of events and data collected during the
test are included in Appendix C. A minor problem, incurring about 2
hours trouble time, occured when the production logging tool (PLT)
assembly had to be retrieved to the surface to replace the
electrical connection at the tool’s head. The test procedure for
this commingled zone was modified to consist of only a major flow
and a major shut-in. The spinner survey was made to determine the
split in the flow rates between the two zones. As the pressure gauge
was in continuous motion during the spinner survey, no bottomhole
pressures were acquired, except later at the end for a buildup. Four
cycles of passes were made with the spinner and a copy of the log is
included in the folder.
The Horner plot of the buildup is shown in Figure 10. A well defined
MTR was established and extrapolating a straight line through this
region yields a p* of 1915 psia at 1312.0 m. (the location of the
pressure gauge for this test). Since it is not possible to relate
this pressure to either of the zones, no attempt was made to correct
this pressure to a datum. The slope of the line yields a
permeability to oi] of 55 md. (Table 7) for the combined zones
(assumed a net thickness of 1.4 plus 3.8m. for the E-24 and £-26
zones, respectively). This corresponds to a permeability to oi] (6 %
water was also produced) of 68 md. for the E-26 zone alone. The skin
factor is essentially 0. A reservoir gradient of 0.33 psi/ft for
the crude and 0.39 psi/ft for water (below 1339.0 m.) were obtained
from the gradient survey at the end of the buildup.

A plot of the Type Curve match is shown in Figure 11. This analysis
yields a permeability (for the combined zones) of 79 md. and a_ skin
factor of 0 (Table 8) which are in reasonable agreement with the
Horner analysis.
